
CURIOUS place this old Island of Manhattan. A
strange, incredible mass of magic rock, so full, ap-
parently, of the eternal creative urge that of its
living self it seems to raise huge towers to the sky. Crop
after crop of structural stone and steel: year after year a
turning in of the lowlier harvest, a sowing and planting for
a larger growth, that in its turn yields ever to taller and taller
plants, reared from a fertile and congenial soil; root, branch,
blossom and fruit-stone-stone and steel.
A stranger from Mars, viewing the stupendous staggering
crop, might wonder at the evidences here and there of infer-
tility, or perhaps of careless reaping. Low spots where the
yield was inferior in every way-in substance, size, color,
durability. Where, inexplicably, there persisted from a for-
mer era an individual plant as out of date in material and in
structure as the little early Tertiary tree-shrew that started
out to become man.
If there had been a Martian stalking unseen along the
streets one rainy night in this year of our era 193—, he could
have found, perhaps, no better place to consider this matter
of unequal growth than the low spot in the darkish furrow
of Twenty-ninth Street, just a little way along from the
broad, light-splashed garden path of Fifth Avenue. For
here the contrast was striking and extreme. Upon the Ave-
nue a church spire of flowered stone, that had once blos-
somed in the sun was eclipsed and shadowed north, south
and east, by towers of materiality so tremendous as to make
9ΙΟ
Counterfeit
of its own aspirations a thing of pity. Only toward the west
was there a hint of breathing room-a space where the orig-
inal relativity between things spiritual and mundane had, so
far, been allowed to persist, at least to the eye. Here, crouch-
ing low behind the baptistery of the church, shrinking back
from the street, sheltering itself from view behind a tall,
dark board fence, was a low brick building almost as dark.
One upper window only showed tiny horizontal slits of light
between the partly closed slats of an old fashioned shutter.
In the solid wooden fence, at the end nearest the church was
an opening defended by magnificent wrought iron gates.
These, quite probably, would have been of no interest to
the problematic Martian, but they obviously were to the taxi
driver who, at about ten o'clock of that rain-swept night of
late fall, drew up before them and peered out inquisitively.
"Guess this is the place, Miss," he said after a minute, as
he pushed the glass slide behind him a little farther open.
"You want I should drive in?" The meter was ticking mer-
rily and there seemed to him no reason for haste. But the
girl he had picked up at the Grand Central apparently felt
differently about it.
"Oh, dear," she cried, (without affectional intent how-
ever), "it's getting so late! And I'm sure this can't be the
place. I expected to be here hours ago. I don't know what
to do. This can't be it. Nobody could live here."
"You said the first door west of Fifth Avenue on the
north side of Twenty-ninth Street. This is it, and I don't
mean maybe," said the taximan somewhat severely.
"This is a gate, not a door," the girl remonstrated. She
was small and rather nicely plump, with determined, large,
steady blue eyes. The cabman had thought his fare "cute"
when he saw her, standing beside her luggage in the bright
light of the station driveway. But there was something,
even in that brief glimpse that warned him as to behavior.
"Well," he admitted, "you're right at that-in a way.
This here's a gate, but the building back in there most likelyCounterfeit

"This would be Number One West Twenty-ninth Street,

the first door"
"Telling me my business," interrupted the driver with a

ought to be Number One. I don't see how it could help itself
without the church had a side entrance, which it hasn't; but
if it had, that would be Number One and not much more
use to a young lady than a gate is, at that."
Merrily, merrily ticked the meter. The rain swished down
with that drenching sound of having no place to go that it
attains only on completely paved streets.
"Can you see any number on the gate?" She was sure
no one could, for she had been forcing her own powers of
sight to the limit. It was rather a preface to a request that
she was not sure she had any right to make. But it was so
awfully wet, and she had just that one good suit. "Or is
there any way you could find out if this is the right place?"
she suggested, trying to sound off-hand and experienced.
The driver proved unexpectedly resourceful. He waited
for a car to pass him then backed a little, swung out toward
the middle of the street and came forward up a sort of drive-
way so as to throw his lights slanting at and through the
gates.
"Holy snakes! What's that?" he gasped. "What kind
of a place you looking for, Miss? Not a grave yard! I
never noticed this here church had one! But if it ain't
a graveyard, then what t'ell is it?”
The immobile, whitely gleaming shapes beyond the gates
looked cold and awful in the pale glare of the car lamps. For

She stopped literally for breath. The old man had ad-
vanced slowly through the rain and now stood glancing in
through the cab window. Beyond the swirling white-gray
shaft of the car lamps the light was dim. She could scarcely
distinguish the expression on the lined face below the float-

"You are glad I came, Uncle Nat?" she whispered hur-
riedly. "Long ago mother used to say that I could always
depend on you. Your letters were so darling and kind. But
we didn't dream it would ever seem right to accept your

"Yes, yes. Oh, yes." The old man's voice was husky,

"Why, Uncle Nat, you said—a home-if I should ever

She was a brave girl but she was very young. Her
voice broke in spite of all she could do. The old man started
as if he had been stung.
"You're all alone?" he muttered. That it was a question
and not an unnecessary statement surprised her.
"Why, of course, Uncle Nat. There's no one now, you
know. I have no one to turn to but you." The last
words were low and there was a note of uncertainty ap-
proaching alarm in the sweet young voice.
"Yes, yes. Quite right." He murmured something
about "Unexpected," "See what can be done," "Perhaps a
hotel-," as he opened the cab door and helped the girl with
her bag and umbrella.

The taxi driver, having received his money, hastened to get

at the gate. The old man, fumbling with the lock, the girl
with bright drops that might be rain on her fresh young

CHAPTER II
OLLOW me, if you will, and watch where you step.
It's rather dark. Mind the curb. There. That's

The old man had carefully relocked the gates and was
leading the way from the ancient cobbled pavement of the
drive, across the flags, toward a small door that could
scarcely be distinguished in the faint glow that filtered in
from the remote city lights.
The girl, half-blindly following her conductor, had to bite
her lip to keep it from trembling. Her father's recent death
had been pure agony. Her almost penniless state would
have filled her with alarm had she not been certain that she
could always find a refuge with this brother of her dead
mother; this uncle who had been a sort of fairy god-father
to her from her earliest childhood, though, owing to a com-
bination of circumstances, this was the first time she had ever
seen him.
. The first time, the very first time he had ever
laid eyes on the niece to whom he had sent every year at
Christmas such a generous check. . . . Even though ob-
viously he had not received her letter and so had no reason
to know she was coming, there was a strangeness and lack
of response in his attitude that was utterly unexpected and
that struck her cold to the marrow. It was more than in-
difference. If it had not been so absolutely absurd she
would have thought he was afraid of her.
Afraid. . . . . . She shivered, and though the old man
seemed wrapped in his own thoughts he was instantly aware
of the tremor that shook her.
"Sorry," he said crisply, in a low voice, as he turned a
key in a grating lock. "Come in. It's warm in here."
Though he did not mention it, it was also entirely dark.
16Counterfeit
17
The girl could form no idea of her surroundings and
waited, with what courage she could command, for the
switching on of a light. But none came. There was the
sound of the door closing behind her, again the complaint of
the key in its rusty wards; a faint, dull thump which she
rightly guessed was made by her bag as it was dropped on
a bare floor. Then her conductor passed her with a few
words uttered in so low a tone that she barely made them
out.
"Sorry," he repeated. "Be back in a minute. Hope you
don't mind the dark. Gas. No matches. Miserable old
hole. Just wait where you are.
Won't be long."
She heard his step pass very quickly along an uncarpeted
floor, then the rapid diminuendo of feet on a stair. Some-
where above a door opened and closed. After a short in-
terval there followed the faint faraway ringing of a strident
bell. Then silence such as she had never been conscious of
before in all her young life. The swish and swirl of the
rising tempest outside, the deep hollow roar of the great city
that never pauses nor goes entirely to rest; the reverberation
that was like the sound in a sea-shell, or the fear of deaf-
ness, in ears accustomed to the voice of the wind and the
song of the leaves, only made the silence of this unexplored
place where she stood the more heart-searching.
But after a time her pride rose to her defence, burning up
the rain and tears upon her cheeks. If it had been possible,
she would have registered her sense of the callous discourtesy
of leaving her there abandoned in the strange darkness by
going out into the driving rain where, at least, coldness
and indifference were to be expected and had the merit of
being impersonal. This grudging admission to a shelter
was like a slap in the face. The few interminable minutes,
that seemed hours, filled her with foreboding, anguish and
a rising anger that for the time subdued her feeling of dread.
If she had had a little more money in her purse, a little
more knowledge of large cities in her mind, she might have18

tried to leave the place then and there. She even went so far
along this line of thought as to grope the few steps back to
the door. Her outstretched hand touched the panel. In-
stinctively she grasped the knob and pulled, though she knew
the door was locked. She felt for the key and something
went cold inside when she realized that it was gone.
Gone! She was virtually a prisoner in a dark house,
thousands of miles away from any one to whom she could
appeal for help,-except her uncle. Surely she had made
no mistake as to his identity. That would be grotesquely
impossible and too awful to contemplate. The address was
right. He looked like the picture he had sent them only a

The darkness became suddenly filled with menace Her
heart pounded in her breast and in her throat She clutched
her courage in both hands. The suspense was unendurable.
There was no retreat. Well, then, she would go forward.
Put it to the touch. Find out where she stood,-figuratively
-literally.
With a determined straightening of her small, gallant
shoulders, she clenched her fists and abandoning her bag
where it lay she took a bold step forward.
And at that instant, from somewhere above and at quite
a little distance, she saw a light flash; a moving light, re-
flected on a wall and revealing in rapidly flickering light and
shade an old-fashioned stair well. For a second the bearer
of the light was unseen, but his footsteps, moving hastily
down the uncarpeted upper flight were distinctly audible.
Then he came into view. It was her uncle, carrying a big
lighted candle in a tall silver holder. For an instant he
leaned over the banister, peering down into the darkness.
The light that he held aloft made an aureole of his great
mass of snowy hair, while at the same time it showed to himCounterfeit
19
Marie Lamont struck to immobility by the suddenness of
the apparition.
He did not speak but came hastily down the last flight of
stairs, and his face, in the wavering candle-light seemed to
take on a kinder aspect, though through everything he said
and did the girl sensed a strange hesitation-a note-she
found herself thinking again-of fear. Fear.
Fear. Of what?
Inexplicably enough, it did seem to be of her. Not just em-
barrassment at having a young girl thrust unexpectedly upon
his hands. It was something other and deeper than that.
"My child." His eyes looked more friendly in the flicker-
ing beam from the candle than she would have thought pos-
sible. He seemed to have shed, with his outer garments,
some of the cold reserve of his former manner, but she was
aware that the glance that rested on her face was keen-
and something more. "You must forgive me if I
seemed abrupt-out there. So unexpected-your coming,
you know. You-you look like your mother, my dear. As
she did when she was your age." He put his hand (it
was trembling slightly) upon her shoulder, drew her to him
and kissed her on the forehead. The caress was, apparently,
sincere and tender, but, the instant it was over, she felt
again that close, inquiring, watchful scrutiny.
"What is it, Uncle Nat?" she cried, suddenly clasping her
hands together. "There's a reason-after all-why you
don't want me! And I was so sure-
""

"I could go away," she interrupted in her turn, trying
desperately to keep her voice quite steady, "but you see I
never was in New York before-and-and I have hardly
any money left."
"If I let you go out again in this storm, my name's not20
Counterfeit
Nathaniel Etherington, and you're not my dear sister Marie's
only child!" he exclaimed with heat. "And that's ridiculous,
for you're as like her as two prints from the same plate.
I'd have known you anywhere. I loved your mother, my
child, and even if I seem old and crabbed, I love you, too.
Please don't doubt it."
He spoke with an irascible, indignant sort of earnestness
that was quaint and somehow very appealing to the tired,
lonely girl. She winked hard to shake off the salt drops
that had gathered on her tangled lashes, and suddenly broke
into a smile.
"Good Lord," cried Nathaniel Etherington, starting back.
"That-that was your mother-to the life. I made a little
picture of her-I'll show it to you... long ago. . . . .
Here, carry the candle," he added, briskly. "I'll take your
bag. I have a little guest room, of a sort, upstairs. You'll
be comfortable for the night at least, and after that," his
hesitation was scarcely perceptible, "we'll see what can be
done to make you more so, my child. I want you to be
quite quite happy, you know. That is as happy as an old
man can make you.'
Without more ado, he gathered up her bag and motioning
her to precede him with the candle, they started up the stairs.
She hesitated before a door on the first landing.
"Not that one, my-my dear," he called out in a hearty
voice. "It's one more flight. Perhaps I'd better lead the
way. This seems a queer sort of place to you, no doubt,"
he went on, quite gaily now, as he passed her and started up
the second and last flight of stairs. "It is an awful hole,
some people think, but when you get used to it it's not so bad.
And there are a great many advantages..

CHAPTER III
ETER CLANCY contended that he was a deeply
religious person, though his friends laughed him to
scorn.
"Bet you haven't been inside a church since your mother,
God rest her soul, passed where I hope there aren't any little
red-headed cherubs to remind her of the imp that worried her
so long," remarked Captain of Detectives Jake Kerrigan as
he helped himself largely from a casserole that had silently
presented itself at his elbow.
"What about your own religion? It's Friday, Jake,"
warned the third man at the table, following the example of
Clancy's other guest. "There's chicken in this, or I'm a
Hindu."
"You may as well be, you and Pete, for all the good there
is to religion for either of you. This is fish, my lad, though
I grant you 'twould be easy to mistake it with all the sauce
there is upon it. 'Tis odd, and I've often remarked it, how
some kinds of fish do taste quite the same as chicken."
"Nothing is, but thinking makes it so," quoted Peter,
laughing.
"And a truer word never was said," Kerrigan agreed,
heartily. "I'll trouble you for the salt, Raymond; if you
please.
""

the same," said Raymond Trant, complying with Kerrigan's

remember, when we were together in Paris the day the
Armistice was signed-Do you recall that, Pete?"
"Yes," said Clancy, gravely.

"Won't you ever stop grouching about that, Jake? Well,
anyway, I'll tell you where Pete went. To the top of the
Eiffel Tower. Sight-seeing, at a time like that! Can you

Kerrigan seemed to be studying the food on his well-
filled plate. "I've seen postcards of that place," he said,
slowly. "It's the highest thing you can get up on in that
sawed-off city, isn't it?"
His fleeting glance met Peter's, and they both smiled.
Between the intelligent police detective and the astute private
one there was an underlying bond of sympathy, deeply valued
by both. Somehow Kerrigan understood why Clancy
wanted nothing over him but the sky at a time like that.
"You could hardly go so far as to say you've got religion,
though, Pete," he argued. Kerrigan's own creed was a
matter of hereditary loyalty, but he considered himself per-
fectly orthodox.
"You wouldn't call it that, I suppose." Peter was half
laughing, half serious. "I haven't any banner to carry or
even a tag to pin on my manly breast, but I've come to be-
lieve certain things." His eyes moved slowly, almost un-
seeingly, around the big, fashionable restaurant where he
was entertaining his two friends. "I've seen so much-of
the way things work-that I don't believe in coincidences any
more. I mean I don't think that it just happens that I've
been here or there or the other place. The stars don't just
happen not to bump into each other . . . . They follow a24
Counterfeit
law. If we could see far enough,—if we had sufficient in-

that everything is the result-and the cause-of

"But you didn't," said Clancy, with a frown and a smile.

to make it a little more snappy.'
""
The waiter, with the air of being intimately concerned
in the matter of their choice, graciously admitted that it was
a good one, and the dinner proceeded.
Kerrigan, who had not previously heard the nature of the
Secret Service man's quest, made enquiries and Trant pro-
ceeded to enlighten him. A marvellously executed counter-
feit one hundred dollar bill had recently made its appearance,
and the Bureau was hot on its trail. Seven notes only had
been confiscated and five of these had been traced-in one
instance immediately and in the other remotely-to widely
separated race tracks.
"As you know, a place like that, where money in wads
changes hands so fast, is a bully good place to pass the
‘queer,'" said Trant, warming to his subject. "The book-
makers are pretty wise, but this note would pass in almost
any bank. In fact it was just by luck that the teller at the
Case National caught the last bill."
"There you go. Luck," said Peter. "The teller had prob-
ably been trained for years, so that he'd practically developed
a sixth sense, and you call it luck!"
He spoke rather absently. For several minutes he hadCounterfeit

HE crackle of the stiff paper sounded loud in the en-
suing stillness. Peter laid aside the three rubber
bands that had held it in place and delicately, with
the tips of his fingers, pressed back the folds and exposed
the contents of the package to view.
Three inconsiderable items. A smallish lump of gray
metallic substance that had been rubbed clean but still showed
flecks of foreign matter inbedded in it. A few inches of
what might have been a piece of pipe-except that no piping,
whether made of clay or metal would, conceivably, have been
charred like that, for it was burned black and was at the
very point of disintergration. The third object showed no
signs of having, literally, been through the fire, but it was
damaged-broken-crushed. A flat pencil holder of gold
with a ring at the top but no chain. It was meant to con-
tain one of those flat lead pencils, the kind that Wiggar
insisted on Peter's using, because they did not cause an un-
seemly bulge in a waistcoat pocket. There was, however, no
pencil in the holder, the flat sides of which were crushed
together until, at the open end, they almost met.
Breathing heavily, Terence Sheehan reached out and
turned the small golden object the other side up. Plainly
marked upon the surface were the initials J. O. O., and in
smaller script the abbreviation, Jr.

a pad and pencil between the two detectives. Peter wrote
rapidly, and Kerrigan, reading as the words appeared, spoke
into the transmitter.
"Late Thursday night or early the morning of Friday the
Thirteenth. From a train somewhere between New York
and St. Louis. Don't know what road. Telegram addressed
to either George Ingalls or Mr. Harden, care John Oliver
Olmstead Company, West Thirtieth Street, this city, and
probably signed John Oliver Olmstead. Read that back.”
Peter went on writing while Kerrigan listened. The room
was very still. Wiggar, like a graven image, waited in the
shadow. Not a gleam of the eye betrayed the fact that he
was tensely alert. After a minute's silence, Kerrigan nodded
his graying head and spoke again into the telephone.
"Another wire from St. Louis Monday the Sixteenth.
Same address, same signature. We want the text of both
messages and the place where they were handed in. Also
what office here received them and transmitted them by
phone."
A few details to insure prompt action and Kerrigan hung
up the receiver.
"That's that," he said, rising and stretching his arms.
"And I thought it was a quiet evening we were to have to-
gether, Pete, my lad, with maybe a game of rummy."
"Instead of maybe a rum game," said Peter with a grim-
ace. He put his hand on the other man's shoulder as theyCounterfeit
59
went toward the door where Wiggar was standing with
Kerrigan's hat and heavy walking stick. "We can't help
the way we're made, you and I, Jake," Peter added quietly.
"Maybe it's mostly curiosity that itches in our brains.
Maybe it's something else. I don't know. But whatever
it is, we'll just have to keep the home fires burning until
Ray Trant gets back from Pondicherry. We'll have to
find out what we can in the meantime, and then, perhaps,
we'll have a few more coincidences to lay before him.T
CHAPTER VII
HE night of Monday the Sixteenth it was that
Terence Sheehan, hugging to his breast the news
that Mr. Jack had arrived safely in St. Louis, had
gone home with a relieved and thankful heart. It was that
same night, but several hours later that Wiggar observed
the entrance of Marie Lamont upon the stage of this strange,
involving drama. And as Terence Sheehan (who had never
heard of her) at the apparent ending of his anxiety, slept
peacefully, so Marie, (who, it seemed, would never be likely
to know of the existence of the little Irish furnace-man,)
felt herself, after her uncle's tardy but sincere welcome, to
be at the end of her troubles, and went quietly and hopefully
to sleep in the little guest room that she had helped him to
prepare for her.
He had insisted, with an odd kind of eager diffidence, in
helping her spread sheets and blankets on her bed. The
things were exquisitely clean and smelled of lavendar. He
laughed at the way the girl sniffed at and enjoyed the old-
fashioned scent. In fact he laughed a good deal and yet
there was an undercurrent of nervousness that Marie found
difficulty in explaining to herself. Her uncle was a "char-
acter," undoubtedly, and she hoped that accounted for every-
thing.
In the morning she woke to a haunting sense of strange-
ness not unmixed with dread. That minute of incalculable
duration, when the ego finds its way back through the con-
voluted mazes of the brain, was heavy with foreboding. It
was not until she had opened her eyes and taken in the
quaint, prim comfort of the room, and the domesticated and
contented appearance of her bits of wearing apparel and
toilet accessories in their new habitat, that the feeling of
60Counterfeit
61
discomfort and uneasiness was partially dispelled. It would
take much to obliterate that first impression last night-the
rainy darkness of the strange city-the more inimical dark-
ness of an empty hall and the more daunting strangeness
of the heavy locked gates and the absence of a key in the
lock of a fastened door.
It was the sound of whiffling and splashing in presumably
cold water in the bathroom next door that wakened her.
She decided with a half smile that no one would make those
noises if the water were warm, and that her uncle was trying
to be quiet for her sake, but found it difficult. She snug-
gled down in the blankets to wait her turn since the apart-
ment boasted but the one bath, and fell asleep again.
The next time she woke, one small, seeking ray of sun-
light had come into the room and was pointing a derisive
finger at the two shoes that seemed to be just stepping off
the edge of a braided rug. They did look very helpless, little
and forlorn, for the bright polish with which they had
started on their long journey was dimmed, like her hopes.
She tried not to think of that. Perhaps it wouldn't be so
bad after all.
The smell of coffee greeted her as she peeped out into the
hall. She could hear her uncle talking over the telephone
in the living-room. Not the words, only the sound of his
voice. There was the draft from an open window some-
where that whisked around her bare ankles and drew the
door to behind her with a sharp bang.
Instantly the telephone conversation ceased and Nathaniel
Etherington came hurriedly into the hall.
"Good morning, my dear," he said. He smiled at the
little tousled, kimono-wrapped figure that turned at his ap-

was questioning and somehow, in a way she could not have
described, vaguely apprehensive.
"Good morning, Uncle Nat." Her clear eyes questioned
in their turn, though he appeared unconscious of the fact.62
Counterfeit
"How will you have your eggs?" he asked with an eager

Realizing that the pronoun feminine in the last sentence

"I'm a very good cook," she put in firmly. "We'd have

T FIRST Marie Lamont could distinguish no liv-
ing human being in the crowded mass of objects
that confused her sight. It seemed as if she must
have stepped backward "Through the Looking-Glass," for
practically everything had its back toward her. Tall ancient
chests, clocks, bureaus, all exhibiting their incontestibly
wormeaten rears. A Niobe mournful to behold, though
showing little but inexcusably sad folds of heavy drapery.
A cast of the Milo Venus, with that lovely gracious slant to
her brave shoulders. A mummy case showing a worn and
broken base where it had lain, who knows how long, in some
forgotten tomb. A prancing faun with a stub of neatly
curling tail to match the pattern of his ambrosial locks. It
was at a point beside his uplifted, gleesome leg that Marie's
disconcerted gaze came to rest.
"How do you do, my dear?"
The small face that appeared for an instant between the
mummy and the faun was alive with intelligence and humor.
Shrewd laughing eyes passed swiftly over Marie's hesitant

penetrating gaze. It was uncanny, and for that fleeting in-
stant quite unnerving.

Peter's muscles were getting cramped. He could shift

himself to change his position otherwise. If he must be
discovered in this ambiguous situation he was determined
that it should be fate and not himself who was to blame.

The man in the window straightened to his full heightCounterfeit
93
and came slowly forward. He moved as one entirely fa-

There was the tiniest click of a key turning in a lock;
the slightly increased light as the door into the hall fell
silently open. A second more. The light vanished. Again
the key—and in the ensuing silence Peter dropped to the
floor, muffled his face in his coat-and found that the tick-
ling imps had left him as suddenly and unreasonably as they
had come.
It had been a bad fright, however. He mopped his fore-
head with a grimy handkerchief before he got again to his
feet. He must spend no more time here, he decided. Only
a second in which to discover what it was that could be seen
from that broken shutter. Like a flitting shadow he crossed
the room and put his dusty red head where the white one had
been only a few minutes before.
Well, there it was. The small paved court or back yard
that Sheehan had told him about. The lower story of the
house in which he stood jutted out beyond the upper ones
and cut off the corner of the court so that the entrance to
the Olmstead building was beyond his range of vision, but
he could see the windows of several floors very clearly.
Directly opposite was an office if one might judge by the
furniture that stood close to the window. Nothing else
could be seen, however, for the sun had found its way into
that narrow crack and the window shades had been pulled
half way to the sill to cut out the glare.
In two of the windows above that, Peter observed the
same sort of semi-transparent screen that was upon the table
in a like position at his own right hand. There seemed to94
Counterfeit
be nothing more of interest so far as he could tell. A work-
man in the opposite building came once and again within
view. And that was all.
Wish I knew more about this business, Peter thought to
himself as he hastily left his post of observation and crossed
over to the work-bench which had focused his attention as
soon as he came into that dusty room. Have to see what
the old bird was looking at, he decided, before I go on my
way... I can't make out what he was up to-slipping
in here so quietly-for all the world as if . .
His thoughts stopped as a car does when the brakes jam.
After a second he said, "Good lord," under his breath. He
had turned his flashlight so that it threw a steady beam down
upon the engraver's bench, where, in the middle of an ord-
erly disorder of small tools lay a rectangle of softly gleam-
ing copper, upon the surface of which, delicately traced with
exquisite precision, was an oval surrounding a half-finished
portrait. All the lines were cut into the plate so that the
image, though shown only in the varying depth of metal,
and without any sort of pigment, was quite recognizable.
The Father of his Country. There was no mistaking the
bold features, the high brow and puffed-out hair.
Breathlessly, with a hand that shook with excitement,
Peter took a small roll of currency from his pocket and
running through it hastily extracted a one dollar bill. He
looked at it long and seriously, and then at the plate upon
the work-bench. The block of metal was slightly wider
and much deeper than the greenback, but the portrait, in pose
and in such detail as Peter's inexperience could make cer-
tain of, was identical. Frowning, he folded the bill in the
center and placed it lightly on the plate. In both dimen-
tions the ovals corresponded to a hair.
Peter drew back and his face turned pale under its grime.
If the person who had engraved that plate had known thatCounterfeit

a stranger was in the room-If he came back now, and

and the Rivermount Press in Boston is printing it. The

any leak would mean a news item. So I still insist that since
Barry, Etherington and myself were all in this shop when
that wire came in- -"He pointed to the paper in Kerri-

it can't possibly be a fake."
The police detective leaned forward on the desk, fixed

hard eye, raised a warning finger and spoke with stern au-

how easily you can explain away the various items that
have been brought to your notice. The police are convinced

they propose to find out what it's all about. The first thing
we come up against is the two bogus telegrams, and believe
it or not, it's bogus they are and no mistake. Therefore,"
he waggled his finger portentously, "the identity of the
sender is what we're interested in first and foremost. On
your own showing, while a number of people could have
sent in the first one, those who knew enough of the facts to
be able to put in the second message were limited to your
chief, you two and the man who is making the plates for
Rockland."

graved plates. Steel plates he'd always supposed. Or were
they sometimes made from copper? So far he'd seen noth-
ing but copper here. He must keep his eyes open. There
evidently was much to learn.

HE DAY wore on toward closing time and Peter
began to watch the clock. He was growing more
and more dissatisfied over the fact that they had
failed to get in touch with Ray Trant. It happened that
the crime of counterfeiting had never before come directly
in his way and he felt terribly hampered by his lack of
expert knowledge. In his place, Trant would have known
exactly what to look for; would not have been oppressed
by the feeling that the answer to the riddle might be lying
somewhere right in front of his eyes. Many of Peter's
most interesting cases had been solved by a brilliant flash
of insight and in a comparatively short time. This looked
as if it might take an unprecedented amount. He had
nothing but a layman's intelligence to go by, but it seemed
to him that the John Oliver Olmstead Company, with its
fine reputation for integrity and the high standard of its
output, together with the character of its personnel and
equipment, was ideally situated for the perpetration of ex-
actly this sort of a fraud, provided the heads of the con-
cern were sufficiently blind . . . involved . . . or-elim-
inated.
Eliminated? Ah. That was the possibility that gripped
most grimly and held Peter, willy nilly, to face a protracted
period of menial labor and days of uninteresting grind, if
that should prove necessary to clear the queer and baffling
situation. If John Oliver Olmstead were suddenly to re-
appear, in unscathed flesh, he told himself, he would turn
the case over to Ray Trant without another thought. In
spite of the queer concatenation of circumstances that had
seemed to him a personal appeal, if it turned out that the
missing man had just gone off on a bat, or for necessary
153154
Counterfeit
relaxation, he would feel himself absolved from pursuing
the merely monetary matter further. Trant would carry
on much better than he. Terry's findings in the furnace,
coupled with the picture of John Oliver Olmstead that had
been emerging to him from various sources during the day,
built up an intriguing enigma that would not let him rest,
and it was a far different matter than Rosen's lure of a
future bonus that had caused Peter to say in all sincerity,
that the old man had furnished him a stronger reason than
ever for wanting "to keep his job."
For among other things, this fact appeared with arrest-
ing clarity. A number of people in the picture would be
benefited materially by Olmstead's death . . . All the old
employees, to a greater or less extent, and of them Ether-
ington, probably, the most. Ingalls would come in for a
fine large slice-and Harden. Harden would benefit most
of all..
And yet here was a bothersome thought. If a man dis-
appeared, and the body wasn't forthcoming, the machinery
of inheritance was complicated and of great deliberation.
Usually a person who committed a murder for gain was in
immediate need. Murder is a desperate business. If a
young man is guilty of such a deed the reward must be at
least presumably close at hand. An older man, paradoxical
as it might seem, would be more willing to wait. He would
not be so stirred by needs or passions and could better force
himself to bide his time. So spoke Peter's experience.
But supposing the motive was, after all, not greed-but
fear? Dread of discovery that might have seemed-might
have actually been imminent. Then the guilty party might
turn out to be anybody. If the partition of the business of
the John Oliver Olmstead Company had nothing to do with
the case, then the riddle of the crushed pencil case, the lump
of silver and the bit of human bone remained a puzzle that
cried aloud for solution.
•
Peter had the maddening, helpless feeling that someone,Counterfeit
155
somewhere, was laughing at his ineffectuality. Even so.
Figuratively he pulled his belt up another notch and grimly
set himself the task of watchful waiting. The unpalatable
rôle he had assumed at least brought him in contact with
many things of general interest, and at all hazards he must
hold it intact. There was a matter directly at hand that he
was still uncertain about and that worried him not a little.
Marie Lamont had not noticed him, probably had not seen
him in the street at lunch time. But in the natural course
of events, both of them being employees of the same com-
pany, they could hardly fail to come face to face. The ques-
tion then was, would she recognize him? What the two
ladies in the antique shop had thought about the gas man
who never came back, he had no means of knowing. He
regretted that more important matters had taken out of his
hands the opportunity of as it were an obvious sponging of
the gas man from the slate, until it was too late. If the
older lady were of the nervous, burglar-scenting type, there
might have been a search, which would have made the
pseudo gas man register more clearly than was at all desir-
able. On the other hand, there was a perfectly good reason
for his not reporting back and troubling them again, since
he could have let himself out, presumably, by the front hall
door. It had been pretty dark in the shop and Peter had
been careful to stand with his back to what light there was.
Unfortunately there had been no opportunity for him to
change his makeup to any extent yesterday morning before
he became an employee of the Olmstead Company. All he
could do was to make his bearing and voice as different as
possible and trust to luck. He would avoid an encounter
with the girl as long as it could be managed, with the quite
reasonable hope that time would very soon erase his image
(supposing that it still persisted) from her mind.
There was another thing that, in spite of his confident
statement to Jake Kerrigan, caused him an uneasiness,
which became more acute as the hands of the clock crept156
Counterfeit
nearer and nearer to closing time. What was Wiggar
doing? In the cold light of day, the amusement to be de-
rived from witnessing the appearance of that perfect being
in a character and surroundings so unsuitable, lost a little
of its charm, and he had spent many minutes of his early
morning toilet arguing about it, but to no avail. Wiggar's
confidence in his own powers was unassailable and his
sense that Mr. Peter should be freed from all bonds of
servitude and allowed to exert his finer talents unhampered
would admit of no denial. "If I cause you any incon-
venience, even in the slightest detail, sir," he said, "I will
submit to any penalty that you may devise." He had made
all necessary plans, he said; he would not explain what they
were except that he would present himself at five-thirty
precisely.
At five o'clock the employees began to leave the building.
No one appeared to be working over-time. The press men
on the top floor were washing up when Peter began his tour
of inspection, camouflaged as it was with a broom and
dustpan. The younger engravers were leaving when he
came down to that floor. Etherington's room was empty
and in the same condition in which Peter had left it. Rosen
opened his door and came out with his hat on just as Peter
finished his observation. Even as the knob turned the new
janitor began plying his brush. The old man responded
pleasantly to Peter's "Good night, sir," and he, too, left
the building.
That floor was empty. Peter shouldered the broom and
went quietly down the stairs. It was twenty minutes past
now, and he had a good excuse for entering the offices, even
supposing anyone were still there. The stairway went
squarely around the grilled elevator shaft and came into the
hall almost directly opposite the door of John Olmstead's
office in which Barry Harden worked. As Peter ran lightly
down the last steps, this door opened suddenly and Marie
Lamont came out. There was no dodging the issue. TheyCounterfeit
157
met squarely, face to face. There was a bright electric light

"Oh," said Harden pleasantly, "Terry says you're taking
hold finely, Pat. Glad somebody turned up that suits the
old boy at last. Will you please open all the office windows
when you sweep, and keep them open until you go home.
The air on this floor is none too good."
The young man had, by accident, stopped in such a posi-
tion that the girl, who had a towel in her hand and was ob-
viously headed for the women's dressing room, was forced
to remain where she was, directly opposite Peter. She
looked up and for a breath-taking instant her glance rested
on his face. Peter stood, square shouldered, straight and
tall, and smiled his best.
"Sure and I'll do that same, sir, and thank you kindly."
he said in an accent never achieved by any whose forebears
have not had their lips touched by the Little People. "It's
a nut I am for fresh air meself, and I hope you'll have no
excuse for complaint wid me work. Maggie's that happy
that I've got me a job at last, and sure, sir, I've promised
the children a lollipop all around come payday."
He need not have made so garrulous a response, but Peter
was nothing if not courageous and thorough. Better put it
to the test. Win or lose. The girl's eyes fell under his gay
gaze, but he felt pretty well assured that there had been no
recognition in them and he breathed again.
Harden was evidently just leaving for he had his hat in
his hand. As Peter moved away down the hall he heard
Harden say, "Could I take you anywhere, Miss Lamont?
I have my car outside."
"Miss Lamont," Peter thought. Not "Marie." Harden158
Counterfeit
wasn't as fast a worker as Ingalls, or else he was not, in

Wiggar! and such a Wiggar! Peter had to look twice
before even his quick eyes had taken in every aspect of
this transformed being. He told himself he should have
suspected the fact that at some point of Wiggar's checkered
and unadvertised career the matter of face makeup might
have claimed this versatile being's attention. For with the
same finish displayed daily in his master's behalf, Wiggar
had developed this new line that fate had thrust before him.
Restraint! Ah, that was the marvel of it. The same sense
of fitness, that would never allow Peter to wear a tie or a
sock even one infinitesimal shade too bright, had guided the
hand that had slightly roughened and darkened the eye-
brows on that calm and wooden mask; had tinted the pale
cheeks, and eke the end of the nondescript nose with the
faint red-brown of a person who lives more by the work of
his hands than by that of his brain. Those hands were
thrust loosely into the pockets of a ragged pair of overalls
that (Peter discovered later) had been procured with
Dick's thrilled connivance, as also the rough, worn shoes,
shirt and coat. The cap was one that Peter himself had
long ago discarded. Every detail of the assumed character
betokened an almost uncanny sharpness of observation on
Wiggar's part. The disguise was perfect but so subtle as to
become the acme of inconspicuousness. As Wiggar, in
proper person, presented no salient point, whether in feature
or figure, to arrest the gaze of a beholder; so in his counter-
feit of a workman, from whom work had been often es-
tranged, he came as near to a representation of vacant space
as a material being might hope to achieve.
And yet, withal, he managed to look smart. Peter could
have laughed aloud. What it was that accomplished thisCounterfeit
163
weird phenomenon it would be impossible to say. It did

But at nine-at ten-at eleven, the police detective re-

Captain of Detectives Kerrigan, was a rather unusual
person for any age or clime. He had grim humor, un-
shakable honesty and intelligence of no mean order, but his
salient quality was a sense of responsibility. What he felt
to be his duty he went through with to the bitter end and
his interpretation of what constituted that often avoided
affair was broad and unrelenting. He was long, too, on
the side of loyalty and friendship and his sense of having
come so near failing old Terry in his need grew keener
as the hours passed.
He had put the matter before his Inspector, who, realiz-
ing that it was distinctly on the cards that the affair might
turn out to be very serious, gave Kerrigan a free hand. In
addition to other considerations, the Inspector would not
find himself inconsolable if his chief of detectives made a
scoop of a case that could then be turned over with justifiable
pride and in an elucidated condition to the Secret Service
Bureau.
With this backing, Kerrigan ordered his conduct of pro-
ceedings. The night before he had warned the patrolman
on the beat that embraced the two buildings, in which his
interest now centered, to keep his eye on them. Nothing
had happened that the man thought sufficiently out of the
way to make a special report necessary, according to his
understanding of the chief's instructions. Kerrigan, how-
ever, was restless and uneasy, and after a busy day, instead
of taking a well-earned rest, as soon as he had finished din-

Peter was troubled about many things as he went ahead
with his work that cold crisp morning, and among the rest
the thought of Etherington's niece occupied a prominent
position. She was so young, so inexperienced, so defense-
less. Her uncle, immersed in his own affairs-whatever-
ah, whatever they might be. And she, not wishing to be a
burden, trying to make her own place in the world. Peter
told himself that he had no real reason to suspect that some-
one might be trying to make a place for her—perhaps a
more remunerative place while it lasted. Ingalls might
easily be carrying on a light, innocent flirtation, or he might
be really and honestly in love with the girl. Anyhow it was
none of Peter's business. It was not his place to warn her
and there was little chance for a man in his apparent posi-
tion to make it effective, even supposing he had the temerity
to try it.
He was thinking this when at about eight-thirty he started

CHAPTER XIX
ETER was almost certain what Jake Kerrigan's
reaction, as soon as he discovered his loss, would
be, and shaped his own course accordingly. After
consulting with and being carefully coached by Terence, he
essayed what might be called a practice game at the front

office. In this position of vantage he was aware of Ker-

street, but having no doubt of his friend's destination, he
incontinently finished his work where he was and swiftly
made his way into the hall, nearly colliding with Marie
Lamont. She had her coat and hat on and had just reached
out toward the elevator bell. It was past lunch time and he
wondered why she was going out at that hour, but he did not
have the leisure, if he had had the inclination, to inquire.
"Beg pardon, Miss," he apologized with his quick,
friendly smile, and passed her with no appearance of haste.
He tapped lightly on the closed door of Barry Harden's

CHAPTER XXI
ETER did not know whether it was his imagina-
tion, the weather, or some deeper cause that seemed
to cast a gloom over all departments of the John
Oliver Olmstead Company that Monday morning. Young
Harden looked worried and was so nervous that he nearly
jumped out of his chair when the sash slipped and banged
as Peter closed the window. One of the girls in the outer
office had been to see her family in the country over the
week-end and had brought back a lot of dried straw flowers
and immortelles. Without giving any reason she had
slipped in early and put a bunch of them on the chief's
empty desk. Perhaps she thought they were expecting him
back by now and the gesture was one of humble welcome,
but the lifeless flowers lying there seemed strangely grue-
some and Peter sympathized with Harden when he asked
Marie Lamont for God's sake to throw them away.
"Not yet," she begged in her sweet clear voice. "It will
hurt the poor girl's feelings. They're really not ugly at
all if they're properly arranged."
She picked the stiff things up and put them in a blue jar
that stood on the old fashioned mantel. A few deft touches
and she stepped back and glanced at Harden who was watch-
ing her rather curiously.
"They're not bad on the mantel," he said. "Leave them
there if you like to have them around."
Both of them had obviously forgotten Peter who was
over by the window polishing an ash tray with commend-
able care.
After a minute Harden said, rather diffidently:
"Do you think your uncle would mind if you went to the
theatre with me again some night this week? It seems a
long time since we saw 'Whether or No.' At least it does
to me."
213214
Counterfeit
"It's nice of you to say so and to invite me again." A
bright color flashed softly up in the girl's face that had
been unusually pale. "I'll be glad to go. I don't have to
ask-anybody's-permission." There was a queer ring
in her voice, as if a little velvet purse full of silver had
dropped on a hard pavement.
If Harden noticed it he only smiled and said, "Free, white
and twenty-one? Just? You don't mind if I mention it
to Mr. Etherington though, do you? And shall we say
Thursday?"
Just then Ingalls came in to ask Marie to take some dic-
tation for him and a minute later Peter put the almost
blinding brass ash tray back on the desk and took his de-
parture.
Etherington was in his work room upstairs for a little
while that morning. He and Rosen were having some high
words when Peter reached the second floor. There had
been a little trouble about one of the steel fire-shutters that
had slipped out of place or stuck or something, and the two
old men seemed to be having a battle royal above the head
of the young chap who was trying to fix it.
It was not the first sporting event of the kind, as Peter
had understood from remarks let drop previously. In fact
the partition that cut in two what had once been one long
workroom had been put in to separate the two old men
and to give greater peace to the remainder of the loft.
There was a conflict of opinion as to which was the ag-
gressor, and in the present instance Peter thought it would
be difficult to determine, Etherington carrying on with
great spirit and Rosen countering with heavy, rumbling
The noise of conflict receded when the general

CHAPTER XXII
ETER had no recollection of how he got back to
the Olmstead Building. His mind was so fixed on
inward considerations that he moved automatically,
and it was not until Sheehan spoke to him as he entered the
furnace room that he came back to the immediate present.
"Lend me this chair of yours, Terry, for a little while,
and don't let anyone know I'm here." He dragged the old
swivel chair over into a hidden, unlighted corner, and
dropped into it, covering his eyes with his hands.
"There's something new, sir?" Sheehan's voice held a
note of alarm. "Glory be! You're not going to be sick, Mr.
Clancy?"
"Careful, Terry. Better call me Pat, even in your mind."
Peter looked around and smiled into the gnome-like,
troubled face. "I've got to think. That's the only thing
that's the matter with me, and that's enough, too. I've
just been romping along, taking notice of all the little pieces
as they showed up, but I haven't tried putting them together,
except in spots. Now I must see what kind of a picture
it's likely to be, because my time may be short here. I
must make hay while the sun shines-or burn up stuff
while there's a furnace handy.
•
""
His voice faded away, and silence fell like a curtain be-
fore him. He sank down low in the old chair, rested his
elbows on the arms, covered his eyes with his interlaced
fingers, and with concentrated purpose allowed himself to
become submerged in the clear deeps of consciousness.
Etherington had become suspicious of Pat Callahan.
Peter was suddenly as certain of that as if an authoritative
voice had told him. Not difficult to imagine how it had
happened. Even supposing that the old man were innocent
223224
Counterfeit
of any special underground methods-if one could still

It was Terry that brought the police into the game-and
at almost the same moment acquired the assistance of a
long overdue and much needed janitor. The two facts, to
an innocent mind, might readily have had no connection.
To a guilty one, if the facts were known, they might
be considered worthy of some investigation. . . . Kerri-

the bets were off, and Etherington was really as good and

Peter was concerned was in the fire, and the term of his

To make the most of these last days-or hours. . . .Counterfeit

The two janitors tried to reassure him. Sam had seven

Etherington did not come back to the Olmstead Company
that entire afternoon, though his absence seemed to be re-
marked by no one except Rosen, whose acquaintance Peter
was carefully cultivating. The jealous old craftsman would
be a more than willing witness for the Government in case
his rival were indicted on a federal charge, and while his
opportunities of observation were somewhat curtailed by the
short partition that separated their respective workshops,
he would still be in a position to supply many technical de-
tails. So Peter, for his own reasons, made himself as solid
in that quarter as possible.
And all the while he listened nervously for a summons
from "higher up." When would he be told that his services
would be no longer required? And above all, who would
tell him? If he were allowed to remain, what would that
mean? That Etherington had not mentioned his suspicions
to anyone?

There being no slightest excuse for remaining, Peter

amount of data, he would set the facts clearly before him,

knowledge and his limitations, what would I do under these

right here, where the proper sort of presses were so ready

probable that he would, at any time, let them go out of his
keeping? He could scarcely carry them about with him.
There would be the front plate with the portrait on it, and

Inconvenient and risky to secrete on one's person . . . What
then?

•
A large drawer full of proofs yielded nothing but an even

A knowledge of the clever hiding places that had been de-
veloped by the criminal mind, was part of the detective's
education. To these he could add many ideas of his own,
but he was careful to consider the matter from his suspected
opponent's point of view.

Something had happened to him in that slow, silent ride
southward on the bumping bus. Something had come
through at last from those unplumbed depths where the sub-
conscious mind collects its observations and impressions.
Peter knew the method, none better, of bringing or rather of
allowing the necessary fact, deduction, or significant detail
to come to the surface of personal consciousness. A calm
mental elimination of all things not germane to the matter
in hand; a concentration that shut out every exterior aspect.
. . If either of the other passengers on the bus had been
watching, they would have thought that the red-headed man
was asleep. But it was then that he saw with startling clear-
ness the thing he had been seeking.
Why should one plate be thicker than another? The ques-
tion shook him to the depths of his being. It had not oc-
curred to him that first day at all. Why should it? He knew
nothing then about the engraving business. There had been
plates lying about, on the presses, the engraver's benches,
and in the shallow drawers where they kept discarded or in-
completed work. Nothing but his habit of infinitely detailed
observation would have made it possible for him to note
so small a circumstance, but now it seemed to fill his mind
to the temporary exclusion of everything else.
A double-thick plate. He could even remember the image
on the smooth copper surface. A picture of upper Fifth
Avenue, with the unmistakable old Vanderbilt mansion in the
foreground. Why should he remember that when his dull
brain had not realized the possible significance. . .
He had reached this point when the bus stopped to let
Wiggar off. From that instant every minute seemed an hour
until he could put this new theory to the test. There might
be some reason, he warned himself, some technical detail of
presswork, that demanded the extra thickness of the metal.
But on the other hand, if his discovery was a genuine one,
it would mean a reconstruction.
The thought still drove him as with whip and spur, asCounterfeit

By this he had dashed through the narrow brick walled

completely masked it from the other side. With quick
caution he peered out around the cold shoulder of a blobby
nymph who, impervious to exposure, was disporting herself
among still more blobby flowers upon the fountain's brim.
Apparently, except for ghostly heroes, gods and god-
desses and the like, the old yard was deserted. Whoever had
collected the incongruous mass of sculpture must have had,
Peter thought, an eye to size rather than quality, to mass
rather than merit. A dozen of the gray, faintly gleaming
objects d'art would have furnished, individually, cover for
any human being, no matter what his stature.
The gates were closed, though whether locked or no it
would be impossible to say. As Peter looked, the lights of
a motor car went quickly by in the street, but inside nothing
stirred.
He paused, perforce, and took his bearings carefully.
Would it be a question of making for the street, or of
doubling back and trying to get into the house? To other
conclusions he had swiftly formed Peter now added this one.
The house was the logical objective.
It was a practical certainty that the front doors, at this
time of night, would be locked. It seemed also obvious that
the man, playing this absurd game of hide-and-seek out
there, would know he would not have time to unlock the door
before Peter would be upon him.
His one chance, Peter thought, is to lure me into a game
of dodging around these ridiculous nymphs and satyrs, and
then somehow to give me the slip and get around to the back
door first. Once inside-

That's funny! Peter was deeply puzzled. There had been

this fantastic setting, Peter would have thought that the
attack was being made by nothing more lethal than a slung-

Though David killed Goliath with a sling and a stone,
Peter considered swiftly, as he warily took shelter behind the
plumper lady who occupied the other edge of the fountain.
He had to crawl to do it without exposing his position, but
it was worth while, for his present protectress, if such she
might be called, commanded a far better view of the grimly
silent enclosure.
Seen from this angle, the strategic piece of statuary would
seem to be an equestrian group that occupied practically the
center of the yard. This had a rectangular base, large, and
about the height of a man. The horse and rider were suf-
ficiently raised to see anything that went on in Twenty-ninth
Street, if such had been their desire. At the minute, Peter
did not envy their position on that account, but because he
did not dare to move forward and leave the passage to the
back court unguarded, he wished he was in the gray stone
rider's place for just an instant, so that he could discover
whether anything was moving on the other side of the base.
Nothing. Nothing stirred. He could not see that dim
corner of the fence so well from here, but had no idea that
his fellow player in the grim game of hide-and-seek would
have stayed long in that somewhat exposed situation. How
he had been able to reach there in the time, Peter could not
imagine. He felt that it was a false move and was prepared
to take advantage of it when the time came. He was glad
of the momentary cessation of hostilities. It gave him time
to think and also to catch his breath.258
Counterfeit
The moon had climbed high overhead by now, and seemed,
in the city's low, diffused radiance, very small and pale, like
a bad water-color that the artist had started to wash out.
Yet, superseded to some extent though she was, the moon
contrived to weave a strange spell among the earth-bound
gods and the mortals of that small section of the curving
surface of the globe. Struck to immobility they all re-
mained, the quick as well as the stone dead, while minute,
measured by thumping hearts, joined minute that would
never come again.
Then suddenly, and without warning, unexpectedly, the
brief armistice was over. Came a queer cry following a
small, low, heavy thud from the back of the pedestal on
which Peter's eyes were fixed. Then a long, black figure
leaped out into the open, and Peter prepared to defend the
passage. His automatic gleamed dully as he raised it and
called: "It's no use! I've got you! Don't you see?"
But the flying figure had seen something of which Peter

Beyond the wall, down across the slanting, dull colored turf,

"Deutschland Ueber Alles!" he cried.
Peter sprang-but too late.
The glass clattered to the floor. Rosen swayed forward
and struck the little table beneath the bold portrait as he
fell.
Peter picked up the glass and held it to his face.
"Prepared for any emergency," he muttered. "An in-
nocent looking liquid-colorless-used for bleaching silver-320

A faint tinkle in the silence. Peter turned sharply. The
kitten had jumped out of her basket, and with velvet paws
was playing with the Iron Cross that had fallen to the floor.
